I guess… work, mostly. I mean, I’ve always had stressful days, but this has felt different. We got a new operations manager, and ever since he started going over the inspection procedures I’ve used for years and picking them apart like I suddenly don’t know how to do my job, I just can’t seem to shut it off when I leave.

And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.

Meetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.

And it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.

At home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.

We got a new operations manager, and ever since he started going over the inspection procedures I’ve used for years and picking them apart like I suddenly don’t know how to do my job, I just can’t seem to shut it off when I leave.\n\nI’ll come home still wound up from meetings, replaying every little comment in my head, and then I’m short with my husband over things that really don’t deserve that kind of reaction. Then I’m lying awake at night going back through what was said, what I should’ve said, whether I missed something. And the next day at work, I’m second-guessing quality calls that used to be routine for me—things I would’ve made without blinking before. It’s just gotten to where I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless about things, or if your main struggle has been more about feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m anxious and on edge, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling hopeless exactly. It’s just that ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work anymore.\n\nI come home wound up, like I’m still in those meetings, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small because I’m already so tense. And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.

Now it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.

So I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.

It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.

I mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.

And being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.

My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.

If I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.

So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.

And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F43.22", "F41.1"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

It’s where my head goes about work.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do have normal worries like anybody does — if there’s a bill to pay or something around the house that needs deciding, I can think about it — but it doesn’t usually take over on its own when there isn’t some reason for it.

Lately, when I catch myself feeling really wound up or second-guessing things, it almost always has a thread back to work somehow. Like I might be making dinner or trying to decide what needs doing at home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said at work, or whether I handled something the right way. So it can look like I’m anxious during everyday things, but in my mind it’s not really about the everyday thing.

I don’t generally sit around worrying about random responsibilities for no reason, or feeling anxious about ordinary decisions if nothing else is going on. It’s more like the work stress keeps following me into those moments.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work, mostly. I mean, I’ve always had stressful days, but this has felt different. We got a new operations manager, and ever since he started going over the inspection procedures I’ve used for years and picking them apart like I suddenly don’t know how to do my job, I just can’t seem to shut it off when I leave.\n\nI’ll come home still wound up from meetings, replaying every little comment in my head, and then I’m short with my husband over things that really don’t deserve that kind of reaction. Then I’m lying awake at night going back through what was said, what I should’ve said, whether I missed something. And the next day at work, I’m second-guessing quality calls that used to be routine for me—things I would’ve made without blinking before. It’s just gotten to where I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless about things, or if your main struggle has been more about feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m anxious and on edge, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling hopeless exactly. It’s just that ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work anymore.\n\nI come home wound up, like I’m still in those meetings, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small because I’m already so tense. And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do have normal worries like anybody does — if there’s a bill to pay or something around the house that needs deciding, I can think about it — but it doesn’t usually take over on its own when there isn’t some reason for it.\n\nLately, when I catch myself feeling really wound up or second-guessing things, it almost always has a thread back to work somehow. Like I might be making dinner or trying to decide what needs doing at home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said at work, or whether I handled something the right way. So it can look like I’m anxious during everyday things, but in my mind it’s not really about the everyday thing.\n\nI don’t generally sit around worrying about random responsibilities for no reason, or feeling anxious about ordinary decisions if nothing else is going on. It’s more like the work stress keeps following me into those moments.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things that aren't related to work, even when there's nothing specific happening that would usually cause you to worry?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I do feel anxious, but when I actually trace it back, it’s almost always tied to what’s been going on at work.

Ever since this new operations manager started, he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore. I’ll come home already wound up, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head or thinking about something he questioned, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small. Or I’ll be trying to relax and my mind goes right back to, “Did I explain that right?” or “Is he going to challenge that decision tomorrow?”

So it’s not like I’m just sitting around worrying out of nowhere about random things. It feels like work follows me home, and then even routine things I used to do without thinking — quality calls I’ve made for years — I start second-guessing the next day. That’s the part that feels new and upsetting.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work, mostly. I mean, I’ve always had stressful days, but this has felt different. We got a new operations manager, and ever since he started going over the inspection procedures I’ve used for years and picking them apart like I suddenly don’t know how to do my job, I just can’t seem to shut it off when I leave.\n\nI’ll come home still wound up from meetings, replaying every little comment in my head, and then I’m short with my husband over things that really don’t deserve that kind of reaction. Then I’m lying awake at night going back through what was said, what I should’ve said, whether I missed something. And the next day at work, I’m second-guessing quality calls that used to be routine for me—things I would’ve made without blinking before. It’s just gotten to where I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless about things, or if your main struggle has been more about feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m anxious and on edge, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling hopeless exactly. It’s just that ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work anymore.\n\nI come home wound up, like I’m still in those meetings, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small because I’m already so tense. And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. Like I might be making dinner or trying to decide what needs doing at home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said at work, or whether I handled something the right way. So it can look like I’m anxious during everyday things, but in my mind it’s not really about the everyday thing.\n\nI don’t generally sit around worrying about random responsibilities for no reason, or feeling anxious about ordinary decisions if nothing else is going on. I mean, I do feel anxious, but when I actually trace it back, it’s almost always tied to what’s been going on at work.\n\nEver since this new operations manager started, he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore. I’ll come home already wound up, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head or thinking about something he questioned, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small. I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me outside of work, no.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work, mostly. I mean, I’ve always had stressful days, but this has felt different. We got a new operations manager, and ever since he started going over the inspection procedures I’ve used for years and picking them apart like I suddenly don’t know how to do my job, I just can’t seem to shut it off when I leave.\n\nI’ll come home still wound up from meetings, replaying every little comment in my head, and then I’m short with my husband over things that really don’t deserve that kind of reaction. Then I’m lying awake at night going back through what was said, what I should’ve said, whether I missed something. And the next day at work, I’m second-guessing quality calls that used to be routine for me—things I would’ve made without blinking before. It’s just gotten to where I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been feeling down or hopeless about things, or if your main struggle has been more about feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m anxious and on edge, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling hopeless exactly. It’s just that ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work anymore.\n\nI come home wound up, like I’m still in those meetings, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small because I’m already so tense. And at night I’m lying there replaying what was said, what he questioned, whether I handled it right. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So… yes, more anxious, more keyed up, like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having the same worrisome thoughts over and over, even when you're trying not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s like the same loop keeps starting over in my head, even when I’m trying to do something else. I’ll be home, and I know I’m not at work anymore, but I’m still thinking about what the new operations manager said, how he questioned the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, whether I explained myself clearly enough, whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Just let it go, you’ve done this job a long time,” but then five minutes later I’m replaying the same meeting again. It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. By the next day, even routine decisions I used to make without a second thought feel like something I have to second-guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with all of this, like feeling restless, tense muscles, or being easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I come home still wound up, still thinking through what was said in a meeting or what might be questioned next. And then it spills into home because I’m tense and distracted, and I end up snapping at my husband over things that probably aren’t really about him. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. It follows me home, and then it affects how I am at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried when you're away from home or separated from your husband, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m away from him, I don’t usually get anxious just because of that. It’s more that if I’m already wound up from work, then I might want to talk to him or I might be more irritable when I do see him, but I wouldn’t say I’m afraid to be separated from him or worried something will happen because we’re apart.\n\nAnd being away from home by itself isn’t usually the issue either. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. So… no, I don’t think it’s about being away from home or away from my husband. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress. I mean, I can go run errands, go to appointments, be out doing normal daily things, and I’m not sitting there worrying because I’m away from home or because my husband isn’t with me.\n\nIf I feel anxious during the day, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to something at work — like replaying a conversation or thinking about what might happen next — not because I’m separated from him. I don’t feel like I need him right there to feel safe or okay. It’s more that when I’m already stressed, I might want to talk to him about it later, or I might be tense when I get home.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me. It’s not the being away from home or him. It’s where my head goes about work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things unrelated to work, like everyday decisions or responsibilities, even when there's no immediate stressor?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do have normal worries like anybody does — if there’s a bill to pay or something around the house that needs deciding, I can think about it — but it doesn’t usually take over on its own when there isn’t some reason for it.\n\nLately, when I catch myself feeling really wound up or second-guessing things, it almost always has a thread back to work somehow. Like I might be making dinner or trying to decide what needs doing at home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about something someone said at work, or whether I handled something the right way. So it can look like I’m anxious during everyday things, but in my mind it’s not really about the everyday thing.\n\nI don’t generally sit around worrying about random responsibilities for no reason, or feeling anxious about ordinary decisions if nothing else is going on. It’s more like the work stress keeps following me into those moments.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling anxious or worried about things that aren't related to work, even when there's nothing specific happening that would usually cause you to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I do feel anxious, but when I actually trace it back, it’s almost always tied to what’s been going on at work.\n\nEver since this new operations manager started, he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, and it’s like I can’t shut it off anymore. I’ll come home already wound up, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head or thinking about something he questioned, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something small. Or I’ll be trying to relax and my mind goes right back to, “Did I explain that right?” or “Is he going to challenge that decision tomorrow?”\n\nSo it’s not like I’m just sitting around worrying out of nowhere about random things. It feels like work follows me home, and then even routine things I used to do without thinking — quality calls I’ve made for years — I start second-guessing the next day. That’s the part that feels new and upsetting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried about being judged or criticized in situations outside of work, like during social interactions or when making everyday decisions in front of others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not outside of work. I mean, I can be self-conscious like anyone, especially if I’m tired or already upset, but I haven’t really noticed myself worrying that friends or people at the grocery store or wherever are judging me.\n\nIf I’m making an everyday decision in front of someone — like choosing something at a store, or talking with a neighbor, or ordering food — I’m not sitting there thinking, “Oh no, they’re going to criticize me.” That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nThe judged-or-criticized feeling is much more tied to work, because that’s where I feel like what I’m doing is being questioned. Outside of that, I might still be a little tense because I’m carrying it around with me, but it’s not like I’m afraid people socially are evaluating me. It’s really more specific than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or worried about being evaluated or performing poorly in situations outside of work, like when you're doing something in front of others or trying something new?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really been a thing for me outside of work, no. I mean, if I’m trying something new, sure, I might feel a little awkward at first — like anyone would, I think — but I don’t usually get caught up worrying that I’m going to perform badly or that people are watching me and judging how I’m doing.\n\nLike, if I’m doing something in front of other people in a normal setting, I might want to do it right, but I’m not sitting there with that same tight feeling of being evaluated. That feeling is much more connected to work for me, where my decisions or the way I do things feel like they’re under a microscope.\n\nOutside of that, I haven’t noticed myself avoiding things or getting especially anxious just because someone might see me make a mistake. It’s not really something I think about much unless work is already on my mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Adjustment Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S081\n  name: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\n  description: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.\n  manifestation: I’m here because ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work—I come home wound up, snap at my husband, lie awake replaying meetings, and then the next day I second-guess routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Symptoms must develop within 3 months of the onset of the stressor\n  manifestation: I was doing fine before the new operations manager came in, and the trouble started building within weeks of his changes to our procedures. By the second month, I knew my reactions to him were affecting me well beyond the workday.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: Within 6 months of the termination of the stressor or its consequences\n  manifestation: This situation hasn’t really ended yet—I still have to report to him and deal with the procedures he changed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It pretty much has a clear cause, as far as I can tell. I don’t usually get anxious when everything is genuinely calm and nothing is happening. It’s more like… work has stopped being contained to work, if that makes sense.

Ever since the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I feel like I can’t just leave it there at the end of the day. I come home already wound up, and then I’m still carrying the whole thing with me. I’ll replay a meeting in my head, or go over something he questioned, or wonder if I should have explained a quality call differently.

And then even at home, when there isn’t anything actually happening right that second, I’m not calm because my mind is still back at work. I’ll snap at my husband over something that really isn’t the issue, and later I know it wasn’t about him. Or I’ll lie awake thinking through conversations from earlier in the day. Then the next day I find myself second-guessing routine inspection decisions I used to make without much trouble.

So it can look like it’s coming up during a quiet moment, but to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels like the work stress is following me into the quiet moments.

It carries into the evening, and I’m wound up enough that I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I feel bad about that too. Then at night it’s worse because there’s nothing distracting me, so I’m lying there going over every comment and every quality call in my head. I mean, I know I feel wound up, but I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to my body to say it’s tense muscles or fatigue in a clear way. I haven’t been sitting there thinking, “Oh, my shoulders hurt because of this,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I notice my mind won’t settle, and then I suppose my body feels… keyed up along with it, but I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed anything really specific or dramatic physically. I think I’ve been so focused on what’s happening at work and replaying all of that that I may not be separating out what’s mental from what’s physical very well.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode where you felt intense fear or discomfort that came on quickly and peaked within minutes, maybe including symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it suddenly comes on really hard and peaks in a few minutes. I mean, I get worked up, and I can feel very keyed up about the work situation, but it’s more like it builds and stays with me, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t had an episode where I thought, “Oh my gosh, I can’t breathe,” or where I suddenly started sweating or trembling out of nowhere. At least, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more of a constant tension in my head, like I’m stuck on the same worry, rather than a sharp attack that comes and goes quickly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, tasks, or people at work because of the stress or worry about how they might go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always been someone who just shows up and does what’s expected, but lately I’ve been pulling back.\n\nMeetings are the biggest thing. If I know the new operations manager is going to be there, or if there’s a chance my inspection procedures are going to be questioned again, I’ll find myself trying to avoid being in the room unless I absolutely have to. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m too busy with the actual inspection work, or I’ll delay responding, but honestly part of it is that I’m worried about how it’s going to go and whether I’m going to get put on the spot.\n\nAnd it’s starting to show in my work record, which really bothers me. I’ve missed some internal deadlines because I keep second-guessing things or putting off the parts where I might have to explain or defend my decisions. That’s not like me. I’ve always been dependable, and now I feel like I’m creating more problems by trying to avoid the stressful parts.\n\nAt home it’s been spilling over too. My husband and I are arguing more, usually over things that probably wouldn’t have turned into arguments before. And I’m not keeping up with our normal evening routines the way I used to — just the regular things we’d do after work. I either don’t have the focus for it or I’m still stuck thinking about what happened at work, so things just… don’t get done the same way.\n\nDoctor: Has the stress and worry you’re feeling been limited mostly to your job situation, or have you noticed similar anxiety in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It started with work, very clearly. I don’t think I was like this before the new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. That’s the thing I keep coming back to, because I’ve done this job a long time and I used to trust my judgment. I’d make routine quality calls and move on with my day.\n\nNow it’s like I can’t leave it there. I’ll lie awake replaying conversations from work, thinking about whether I said the wrong thing or whether one of my decisions is going to get challenged.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m anxious about everything in life in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the job situation. But it doesn’t stay neatly at work anymore, if that makes sense. Not really in that way.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being on my own during the day, and I don’t feel like I can’t manage if I’m not at home or if my husband isn’t right there. If I’m out running errands or doing normal things, I don’t notice that same kind of worry unless my mind drifts back to work. It’s more connected to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the stress you’re feeling about work, have you noticed yourself feeling anxious or worried when you’re away from home or separated from your husband, even during normal daily routines?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven’t noticed that outside of the work stress.
